. ∎

3
Semisorting and Reduction to Semisorted Functions

As we mentioned earlier when we stated (
H1
), we do not know if this is a true statement for an arbitrary function.
It is true for what we call semisorted functions, and proving this would be the bulk of the work. In this section, we define what semisorted
functions are, we prove that the Talagrand objective can only decrease when one moves to a semisorted function, and therefore how one can reduce to proving
Theorem

1.4

only for semisorted functions.

Fix a function
f:[n]^{d}\to\{0,1\}
. Fix a coordinate
i
and fix an interval
I=[a,b]
.
Semisorting
f
on this interval in dimension
i
leads to a function
h:[n]^{d}\to\{0,1\}
as follows. We take every
i
-line
\ell

and consider the function restricted on the interval
I
on this line, and we sort it. The following lemma shows that semisorting on any
(i,I)
pair
can only reduce the Talagrand objective. We defer its proof to
Section

3.1
.

Lemma 3.1

(Semisorting only decreases
T_{\Phi}
.)
.

Let
f
be any hypergrid function and let
\chi
be any bicoloring of the augmented hypergrid edges. Let
i\in[d]
be any dimension and
I
be any interval
[a,b]
.
There exists a (re)-coloring
\chi^{\prime}
of the edges of the augmented hypergrid such that

T_{\Phi_{\chi}}(f)\geq T_{\Phi_{\chi^{\prime}}}(h)

where
h
is the function obtained upon semisorting
f
in dimension
i
on the interval
I
.

A function
f:[n]^{d}\to\{0,1\}
is called
semisorted
if for any
i\in[d]
and any
i
-line
\ell
, the function restricted to the first
n/2
points is sorted increasing
and the function restricted to the second half is also sorted increasing. It is instructive to note that when
n=2
, that is when the domain is the hypercube, every function is semisorted.
This shows that semisorted functions form a non-trivial family. However, the semisortedness is a property that allows us to prove that
(
H1
) holds. In particular, we prove this theorem.

Theorem 3.2

(Connecting Talagrand Objectives of
f
and Tracker Functions)
.

Let
f\colon[n]^{d}\to\{0,1\}
be a semisorted function and let
\chi\colon E\to\{0,1\}
be an arbitrary coloring of the edges of the fully augmented hypergrid.
Then for every
\mathbf{x}\in[n]^{d}
, one can find a coloring
\xi_{\mathbf{x}}
of the edges of the Boolean hypercube such that

T_{\Phi_{\chi}}(f):=\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{x}\in[n]^{d}}~\left[\sqrt{\Phi_{f,\chi}(\mathbf{x})}\right]~~\geq~~\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{x}\in[n]^{d}}~\mathbf{E}_{S\subseteq[d]}~~[\sqrt{I_{g_{\mathbf{x}},\xi_{\mathbf{x}}}(S)}]\text{.}

We can use the above theorem to get set the intuition behind (
H2
) correct, and prove
Theorem

1.4
for semisorted functions. We state this below, but we defer the proof
of this to
Section

4
. At this point we remind the reader again that this is not at all trivial, but the proof ideas are generalizations of those present in
[
KMS18
,
PRW22
]

for the hypercube case.

Theorem 3.3

(
Theorem

1.4
for semisorted functions.)
.

Let
f:[n]^{d}\to\{0,1\}
be a semisorted function that is
\varepsilon
-far from monotone. Let
\chi:E\to\{0,1\}
be an arbitrary coloring of the edges of the augmented hypergrid.
Then there is a constant
C^{\prime\prime}
such that

T_{\Phi_{\chi}}(f):=\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{x}}~\left[\sqrt{\Phi_{f,\chi}(\mathbf{x})}\right]\geq C^{\prime\prime}\varepsilon

Lemma

3.1
shows that the Talagrand objective can’t rise on semisorting. The distance to monotonicty, however, can fall. In the remainder of the section we show
how we can reduce to the semisorted case with a loss of
\log n
, and in particular, we use
Theorem

3.3
to prove
Theorem

1.4
.

Sequence of Semisorted Functions and Reduction to the Semisorted Case.

We now describe a semi-sorting process which gives a way of getting from
f
to a monotone function. Without much loss of generality, let us assume
n=2^{k}
which we can assume by padding.
Iteratively coarsen the domain
[n]^{d}=[2^{k}]^{d}
as follows. First “chop” this hypergrid into
2^{d}
many
[n/2]^{d}=[2^{k-1}]^{d}
hypergrids by slicing through the “middle” in each of the
d
-coordinates.
More precisely, these
2^{d}
hypergrids can be indexed via
\mathbf{v}\in\{0,1\}^{d}
, where given such a vector, the corresponding hypergrid is

H_{\mathbf{v}}=\prod_{i=1}^{d}\{\mathbf{v}_{i}\cdot\frac{n}{2}+1,\mathbf{v}_{i}\cdot\frac{n}{2}+2,\cdots,\mathbf{v}_{i}\cdot\frac{n}{2}+\frac{n}{2}\}

Each hypergrid
H_{\mathbf{v}}
is an
[n/2]^{d}=[2^{k-1}]^{d}
hypergrid. Let us denote the collection of all these hypergrids as the set
{\cal H}_{1}
.
So,
{\cal H}_{1}
has
2^{d}
many hypergrids and each hypergrid has dimension
[n/2]^{d}=[2^{k-1}]^{d}
.
Repeat the above operation on each hypergrid in
{\cal H}_{1}
. More precisely, each hypergrid
H_{\mathbf{v}}
in
{\cal H}_{1}
will lead to
2^{d}
hypergrids each with dimension
[n/4]^{d}=[2^{k-2}]^{d}
.
The total number of such hypergrids, which we collect in the collection
{\cal H}_{2}
, is
2^{d}\times 2^{d}=(2^{2})^{d}
. More generally, we have a family
{\cal H}_{i}
consisting of
\left(2^{i}\right)^{d}
many
hypergrids of dimension
[n/2^{i}]^{d}=[2^{k-i}]^{d}
. The collection
{\cal H}_{k-1}
consists of
(2^{k-1})^{d}
many
d
-dimensional hypercubes.

Figure 2:
In the figure, we see an example with
d=2
and
n=8=2^{3}
. There are
2^{2}
many
4\times 4
green (hyper)-grids,
and
4^{2}
many
2\times 2
red squares.

Note that in any family
{\cal H}_{i}
for
1\leq i\leq k-1
, each
H\in{\cal H}_{i}
is a sub-hypergrid of
[n]^{d}
. We let
f_{H}
denote the restriction of
f
only to this subset
H
of the domain.
Also, let
{\cal H}_{0}
denote the singleton set containing only one hypergrid,
[n]^{d}
.
Define the function
f_{1}:[n]^{d}\to\{0,1\}
as follows: consider every hypergrid
2
2

2

these will be hypercubes

H
in
{\cal H}_{k-1}
and apply the sort operator on
f_{H}
for all these hypergrids. Note that
f_{1}
is a monotone function when restricted to
H\in{\cal H}_{k-1}
. Recursively define
f_{i}
as follows: consider every hypergrid
H\in{\cal H}_{k-i}
and apply the sort operator on
(f_{i-1})_{H}
for all these hypergrids.

Figure

3
is an illustration for
d=2
and
k=3
, i.e.
n=8
.

Figure 3:
The function
f=f_{0}
is described to the left, and then one obtains
f_{1},f_{2}
and
f_{3}
. The function
h
which is obtained doing sort on the whole of
f

is described below. Note
h\neq f_{3}
.

Claim 3.4
.

There must exist an
0\leq j\leq k-1
such that
\Delta(f_{j},f_{j+1})\geq\varepsilon_{f}/k
.

Proof.

This follows from triangle inequality and the fact that
\Delta(f_{0},f_{k})\geq\varepsilon_{f}
.
∎

Proof of
Theorem

1.4
.

We now show how
Theorem

1.4
follows from
Lemma

3.1
and
Theorem

3.3
via an averaging argument.
We fix the
j
as in
3.4
.
By
Lemma

3.1
we get that for any function
f
and any coloring
\chi
, there exists a recoloring
\chi^{\prime}
such that

T_{\Phi_{\chi}}(f)\geq T_{\Phi_{\chi^{\prime}}}(f_{j})
. Now consider the hypergrids in
H\in{\cal H}_{k-j-1}
. Let
f_{j}|_{H}
be the function restricted to this sub-domain
H
.
Note that the function
f_{j}|_{H}
is indeed semisorted by construction. Therefore, by
Theorem

3.3
(on the coloring
\chi^{\prime}
) we know that for all
H\in{\cal H}_{k-j-1}
,

T_{\Phi_{\chi^{\prime}}}({f_{j}|_{H}})\geq C^{\prime\prime}\cdot\varepsilon_{f_{j}|_{H}}

By
2.5
, we know that
2\varepsilon_{f_{j}|_{H}}\geq\Delta(f_{j}|_{H},f_{j+1}|_{H})
. Taking expectation over
H\in{\cal H}_{k-j-1}
,
we see that the LHS is at most (at most since we only consider violations staying in
H
)
T_{\Phi_{\chi^{\prime}}}(f_{j})
, while the RHS is precisely
\Delta(f_{j},f_{j+1})/2\geq\varepsilon_{f}/2k
.
Putting everything together, we get

T_{\Phi_{\chi}}(f)\geq\frac{C^{\prime\prime}\varepsilon_{f}}{2\log n}

proving
Theorem

1.4
.
∎

3.1
Semisorting only decreases the Talagrand objective: Proof of
Lemma

3.1

Let us first describe the coloring
\chi^{\prime}
.

First let us describe the recoloring of pairs of points
(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{x}^{\prime})

j\neq i

and
\mathbf{x}_{i}=\mathbf{x}^{\prime}_{i}
lies in the interval
[a,b]
. We go over all these edges by considering pairs of

-lines which differ on a single coordinate

.
More precisely, if

\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr

(1)

coordinates of

\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr

\hfil\textstyle\Phi_{\chi}(f)\hfil

into
O\cup Z
corresponding to when
f(\mathbf{x})=1
and
f(\mathbf{x})=0
. Let’s call these two vectors

\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr

\hfil\textstyle\Phi^{(1)}_{\chi}(f)\hfil

and

\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr

\hfil\textstyle\Phi^{(0)}_{\chi}(f)\hfil

. The former vector is
|O|
dimensional, the latter is
|Z|
dimensional, and

\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr

\hfil\textstyle\Phi_{\chi}(f)\hfil

is obtained by some splicing of these two vectors.
We will do the same for the coordinates of

\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr

\hfil\textstyle\Phi_{\chi^{\prime}}(h)\hfil

to obtain

\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr

\hfil\textstyle\Phi^{(1)}_{\chi^{\prime}}(h)\hfil

and

\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr

\hfil\textstyle\Phi^{(0)}_{\chi^{\prime}}(h)\hfil

. Note that since sorting doesn’t change the number of
0
s or
1
,
both these vectors are
|O|
and
|Z|
dimensional, respectively.

(2)

and this will prove (

). We prove the first inequality; the proof of the second is analogous. For brevity’s sake, for the rest of the section we drop the superscript
(1)
from

\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr

.

The plan is to write

\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr

\hfil\textstyle\Phi_{\chi}(f)\hfil

as a sum of (Boolean) vectors, and then show that

\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr

\hfil\textstyle\Phi_{\chi^{\prime}}(h)\hfil

is dominated by the sum of sorts of those Boolean vectors. Then we invoke
Lemma

2.11
.

We write

\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr

\hfil\textstyle\Phi_{\chi}(f)\hfil

as a sum of Boolean vectors as follows.
Fix any other
i
-line
\ell^{\prime}:=\ell+a\mathbf{e}_{j}
for some
j\neq i
and
a>0
. Define the following

(0,1)
-vector also indexed by elements of
O
.

\mathbf{u}_{\ell^{\prime}}(\mathbf{x})=1~~\text{if $f(\mathbf{x}+a\mathbf{e}_{j})=0$ and $\chi(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{x}+a\mathbf{e}_{j})=1$}

That is,
\mathbf{u}_{\ell^{\prime}}(\mathbf{x})=1
if the projection of
\mathbf{x}
onto
\ell^{\prime}
,
(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{x}^{\prime}:=\mathbf{x}+a\mathbf{e}_{j})
, is a violating edge in
f
with
\chi
-color
1
.

Define the following vector

\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr

\hfil\textstyle A^{\bot}\hfil

as follows.

.

.

\mathbf{x}\in O
,

2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle A^{\parallel}\hfil$\crcr}}}(\mathbf{x})

Now let’s consider the situation after
I
is sorted. The ones of
O
now “shift around”; indeed, they are the
|O|
many right most points.
Let’s call these locations
O^{\prime}
and note
|O^{\prime}|=|O|
.

Now define the
|O^{\prime}|=|O|
dimensional vector
\mathbf{v}_{\ell^{\prime}}
where for
\mathbf{x}\in O^{\prime}

\mathbf{v}_{\ell^{\prime}}(\mathbf{x})=1~~\text{if $h(\mathbf{x}+a\mathbf{e}_{j})=0$ and $\chi^{\prime}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{x}+a\mathbf{e}_{j})=1$}

Now we will use the property of the recoloring we performed. We claim two things:

Claim 3.7
.

The number of
1
s in
\mathbf{v}_{\ell^{\prime}}
is at most the number of
1
s in
\mathbf{u}_{\ell^{\prime}}
, and
\mathbf{v}_{\ell^{\prime}}
is sorted decreasing.

Proof.

The number of
1
s in
\mathbf{u}_{\ell^{\prime}}
is precisely the number of violating edges of the form
(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{x}^{\prime})
in
f
, where
\mathbf{x}_{i}\in I
and
\mathbf{x}^{\prime}=\mathbf{x}+a\mathbf{e}_{j}
and
\chi(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{x}^{\prime})=1
.
Similarly, the number of
1
s in
\mathbf{u}_{\ell^{\prime}}
are precisely the number of violating edges of the form
(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{x}^{\prime})
in
h
, where
\mathbf{x}_{i}\in I
and
\mathbf{x}^{\prime}=\mathbf{x}+a\mathbf{e}_{j}
and
\chi^{\prime}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{x}^{\prime})=1
.
When we recolored to get
\chi^{\prime}
we made sure by property (a) that the latter number is smaller.

Take
\mathbf{x}
and
\mathbf{y}
in
O
, with
\mathbf{x}_{i}<\mathbf{y}_{i}
, but suppose, for the sake of contradiction,
\mathbf{v}_{\ell^{\prime}}(\mathbf{x})=0
and
\mathbf{v}_{\ell^{\prime}}(\mathbf{y})=1
.
The latter implies
h(\mathbf{y}^{\prime}:=\mathbf{y}+a\mathbf{e}_{j})=0
and
\chi^{\prime}(\mathbf{y},\mathbf{y}^{\prime})=1
. Since
h
is sorted on
\ell^{\prime}
,
h(\mathbf{x}^{\prime}:=\mathbf{x}+a\mathbf{e}_{j})=0
as well.
Since
\mathbf{x}\in O
,
h(\mathbf{x})=1
which means
(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{x}^{\prime})
is a violating edge in
h
.
\mathbf{v}_{\ell^{\prime}}(\mathbf{x})=0
implies
\chi^{\prime}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{x}^{\prime})=0
.
But this violates property (b) of
\chi^{\prime}
.
∎

What we need is the following corollary.

\textrm{For any $\ell^{\prime}=\ell+a\mathbf{e}_{j}$},~~~\mathbf{v}_{\ell^{\prime}}~\leq_{\mathrm{coor}}\left(\mathbf{u}_{\ell^{\prime}}\right)^{\downarrow}

(3)

where recall that
\left(z\right)^{\downarrow}
is the sorted-decreasing version of
z
.

Just as we defined

\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr

\hfil\textstyle A^{\bot}\hfil

, define the
|O|
-dimensional vector

\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr

\hfil\textstyle B^{\bot}\hfil

as follows.

Definition 3.8
.

For any
\mathbf{x}\in O^{\prime}
,

\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern i}\mathbf{z}_{j}(\mathbf{x})

Note that for every
j\neq i
,
\mathbf{w}_{j}
and
\mathbf{z}_{j}
are
|O|=|O^{\prime}|
dimensional Boolean vectors which we index by
\mathbf{x}\in O
and
\mathbf{x}\in O^{\prime}
, respectively.

Claim 3.9
.

For all
j
,
\mathbf{z}_{j}\leq_{\mathrm{coor}}\left(\mathbf{w}_{j}\right)^{\downarrow}
.

Proof.

Follows from (
3
), and the defintions of
\mathbf{z}_{j}
and
\mathbf{w}_{j}
as described in
Definition

3.5
and
Definition

3.8
.
∎

\mathbf{x}\in O^{\prime}
, define the

dimensional vector

as

2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle B^{\parallel}\hfil$\crcr}}}~~~\leq_{\mathrm{coor}}~~~\underbrace{\sum_{j\neq i}\left(\mathbf{w}_{j}\right)^{\downarrow}+\left(\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\displaystyle\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle A^{\parallel}\hfil$\crcr}}}\right)^{\downarrow}}_{\text{call this $\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\displaystyle\mkern Trivially, we have
\left\lVert\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptstyle\Phi_{\chi^{\prime}}(h)\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle\Phi_{\chi^{\prime}}(h)\hfil$\crcr}}}\right\rVert_{1/2}\leq\left\lVert\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\textstyle s\Phi\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle s\Phi\hfil$\crcr}}}\right\rVert_{1/2}
, and from
Lemma

2.11
, we get
\left\lVert\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\displaystyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle s\Phi\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\textstyle s\Phi\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptstyle s\Phi\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle s\Phi\hfil$\crcr}}}\right\rVert_{1/2}\leq\left\lVert\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\displaystyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle\Phi_{\chi}(f)\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\textstyle\Phi_{\chi}(f)\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptstyle\Phi_{\chi}(f)\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle\Phi_{\chi}(f)\hfil$\crcr}}}\right\rVert_{1/2}
, completing the proof of the first part of (
2
).

4
Connecting with the Distance to Monotonicity: Proof of
Theorem

3.3

In this section, we set the intuition behind (
H2
) straight. We show how the isoperimetric theorem
Theorem

3.2

on semisorted functions can be used to prove
Theorem

3.3
.
We begin by recalling the corollary of the undirected, colored Talagrand
objective on the hypercube.
See
1.9

As mentioned earlier, one can’t show (
H2
), that is,

\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{x}}[\mathrm{var}(g_{\mathbf{x}})]=\Omega(\varepsilon_{f})
. Indeed, there are examples
of functions even over the hypercube where the above bound does
not
hold.
KMS deal with this problem by applying
Theorem
3.2

to random restrictions of
f
. One can show that there
is some restriction where the corresponding
\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{x}}[\mathrm{var}(g_{\mathbf{x}})]
is large. They referred
to these calculations as the “telescoping argument”. This argument was quantitatively improved
by Pallavoor-Raskhodnikova-Waingarten
[
PRW22
]
.

In this section, we port that argument to the hypergrid setting. Our proof
is different in its presentation, though the key ideas are the same as KMS.
Our first step is to convert
Theorem
3.2
to a more convenient form,
using the undirected
Theorem
2.8
.

Theorem 4.1
.

There exists a constant
C^{\prime}>0
such that for any
semisorted function
f:[n]^{d}\to\{0,1\}
and any arbitrary coloring
\chi:E\to\{0,1\}
of the augmented hypergrid, we have

T_{\Phi_{\chi}}(f)\geq C^{\prime}\cdot\mathbf{E}_{S}[\Delta(S\circ f,\overline{S}\circ f)]\text{.}

Proof.

By
Theorem
3.2
, there exists some colorings
\xi_{\mathbf{x}}
such that
T_{\Phi_{\chi}}(f)\geq\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{x}}\mathbf{E}_{S}[\sqrt{I_{g_{\mathbf{x}},\xi_{\mathbf{x}}}(S)}]
.
By the undirected Talagrand bound
Theorem
2.8
,

\mathbf{E}_{S}[\sqrt{I_{g_{\mathbf{x}},\xi_{\mathbf{x}}}(S)}]\geq C\cdot\mathrm{var}(g_{\mathbf{x}})
.

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}_{S}[\Delta(S\circ f,\overline{S}\circ f)]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}_{S}\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{x}}[\mathbbm{1}((S\circ f)(\mathbf{x})\neq(\overline{S}\circ f)(\mathbf{x}))]

(4)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}_{S}\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{x}}[\mathbbm{1}(g_{\mathbf{x}}(S)\neq g_{\mathbf{x}}(\overline{S}))]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{x}}\mathbf{E}_{S}[\mathbbm{1}(g_{\mathbf{x}}(S)\neq g_{\mathbf{x}}(\overline{S}))]\leq 4\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{x}}[\mathrm{var}(g_{\mathbf{x}})]

(The final inequality uses
Claim
4.2
, stated below.)
Hence,
\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{x}}\mathbf{E}_{S}[\sqrt{I_{g_{\mathbf{x}},\xi}(S)}]\geq(C/4)\mathbf{E}_{S}[\Delta(S\circ f,\overline{S}\circ f)]
.

Claim 4.2
.

For any Boolean function
h:\{0,1\}^{d}\to\{0,1\}
,
\Pr_{S}[h(S)\neq h(\overline{S})]\leq 4\mathrm{var}(h)
.

Proof.

Recall that
\mathrm{var}(h)=4\Pr_{S}[h(S)=0]\Pr_{S}[h(S)=1]
.
Hence,
\mathrm{var}(h)=4\max_{b\in\{0,1\}}\Pr_{S}[h(S)=b]\min_{b\in\{0,1\}}\Pr_{S}[h(S)=b]
.
Since one of the values is taken with probability at least
1/2
,
\mathrm{var}(h)\geq 2\min_{b\in\{0,1\}}\Pr_{S}[h(S)=b]
.

Let
\bm{S}=\{S\ |\ h(S)\neq h(\overline{S})\}
. Observe that half the sets in
\bm{S}
have an
h
-value of
1
,
and the other half have value zero. Hence,
\Pr_{S}[h(S)\neq h(\overline{S})]\leq 2\min_{b\in\{0,1\}}\Pr_{S}[h(S)=b]
.
Combining with the bound from the previous paragraph,
\Pr_{S}[h(S)\neq h(\overline{S})]\leq 4\mathrm{var}(h)
.
∎

∎

We now give some definitions and claim regarding the Talagrand objective of random restrictions
of functions.

Definition 4.3
.

Let
S\subseteq[d]
be a subset of coordinates.
The
distribution of restrictions on
S
, denoted
{\cal R}_{S}
, is supported over functions and generated
as follows. We pick a uar setting of the coordinates in
\overline{S}
, and output the function under this restriction.
(Hence,
h\sim{\cal R}_{S}
has domain
[n]^{S}
.)

The isoperimetric theorem of
Theorem
3.2
holds for any
ordering of the coordinates. In this section, we will need to randomize the ordering
of the sort operators.
We will represent an ordering
as a permutation
\pi
over
[d]
. Abusing notation, for any subset
S\subseteq[d]
,

\pi(S)
is the induced ordered list of
S
.

Definition 4.4
.

For any function
h:[n]^{k}\to\{0,1\}
, define
\delta(h)

to be
\mathbf{E}_{\pi}[\Delta(h,\pi([k])\circ h)]
.

By
Claim
2.3
, sorting on all coordinates leads to a monotone function.
Thus,
\delta(h)
is at least the distance of
h
to monotonicity.
We will perform our analyses in terms of
\delta(f)
, since it is more
amenable to a proof by induction over domain size.

The following claim is central to the final induction, and relates
\delta(f)

to
\mathbf{E}_{S}[\Delta(S\circ f,\overline{S}\circ f)]
. This is the (only) claim
where we need to permute the coordinates. All other claims and theorems
hold for an arbitrary ordering of the coordinates (when defining
S\circ f
).

Claim 4.5
.

\delta(f)\leq\mathbf{E}_{S}\mathbf{E}_{h\sim{\cal R}(S)}[\delta(h)]+\mathbf{E}_{\pi}\mathbf{E}_{S}[\Delta(\pi(S)\circ f,\pi(\overline{S})\circ f)]

Proof.

Let us consider an arbitrary ordering of dimensions.
By triangle inequality,

\displaystyle\Delta(f,S\circ\overline{S}\circ f)\leq\Delta(f,S\circ f)+\Delta(S\circ f,S\circ\overline{S}\circ f)

Observe that
S\circ S\circ f=S\circ f
, since sorting repeatedly on a dimension does not modify a function.
Hence,
\Delta(S\circ f,S\circ\overline{S}\circ f)=\Delta(S\circ S\circ f,S\circ\overline{S}\circ f)\leq\Delta(S\circ f,\overline{S}\circ f)
. The latter inequality holds because sorting only reduces the Hamming distance
between functions (
Claim
2.4
). Plugging this bound in and taking expectations over ordered subset
S
of dimensions:

\mathbf{E}_{S}[\Delta(f,S\circ\overline{S}\circ f)]\leq\mathbf{E}_{S}[\Delta(f,S\circ f)]+\mathbf{E}_{S}[\Delta(S\circ f,\overline{S}\circ f)]

(5)

Observe that
S\circ f
only changes the function in the dimensions in
S
, and can be thought
to act on the restrictions of
f
(to
S
). Hence
\mathbf{E}_{S}[\Delta(f,S\circ f)]=\mathbf{E}_{h\sim{\cal R}(S)}[\Delta(h,S\circ h)]
.
Roughly speaking, the quantity
\Delta(f,S\circ\overline{S}\circ f)
is
\varepsilon(f)

and
\mathbf{E}_{h\sim{\cal R}(S)}[\Delta(h,S\circ h)]
is
\mathbf{E}_{h\sim{\cal R}{S}}\varepsilon(h)
. So we would
hope that
(
5
)
implies
\varepsilon(f)\leq\varepsilon(h)+\mathbf{E}_{S}[\Delta(S\circ f,\overline{S}\circ f)]
.

Unfortunately, the quantities are only constant factor approximations of
\varepsilon(f),\varepsilon(h)
.
So by converting
(
5
)
in terms of
\varepsilon(f)
, we would potentially lose a constant factor
in
(
5
)
.

To avoid this problem, we deal with
\delta(f)
instead. By randomly permuting
S
and taking expectations,
the quantities in
(
5
)
can be replaced by
\delta(\cdot)
terms.
Taking expectations over a uar
\pi
,
(
5
)
implies

\mathbf{E}_{\pi}\mathbf{E}_{S}[\Delta(f,\pi(S)\circ\pi(\overline{S})\circ f)]\leq\mathbf{E}_{\pi}\mathbf{E}_{S}[\Delta(f,\pi(S)\circ f)]+\mathbf{E}_{\pi}\mathbf{E}_{S}[\Delta(\pi(S)\circ f,\pi(\overline{S})\circ f)]

(6)

Note that the switching order in the LHS,
\pi(S)\circ\pi(\overline{S})
, is uniformly random.
Moreover,

\mathbf{E}_{\pi}\mathbf{E}_{S}\mathbf{E}_{h\sim{\cal R}(S)}[\Delta(h,\pi(S)\circ h)]=\mathbf{E}_{S}\mathbf{E}_{h}\mathbf{E}_{\pi}[\Delta(h,\pi(S)\circ h)]=\mathbf{E}_{S}\mathbf{E}_{h}[\delta(h)]

Combining all our bounds, we get that
\delta(f)\leq\mathbf{E}_{S}\mathbf{E}_{h\sim{\cal R}(S)}[\delta(h)]+\mathbf{E}_{\pi}\mathbf{E}_{S}[\Delta(\pi(S)\circ f,\pi(\overline{S})\circ f)]
.
∎

We prove a useful claim about the Talagrand objective of restrictions, made in
[
PRW22
]
.

Claim 4.6
.

Let
p\in(0,1)
, and
{\cal H}(p)
be the distribution
of subsets of
[d]
generated by selecting each element with iid probability
p
. Then,

T_{\Phi_{\chi}}(f)\geq(1/\sqrt{p})\cdot\mathbf{E}_{S\sim{\cal H}(p)}\mathbf{E}_{h\sim{\cal R}_{S}}[T_{\Phi_{\chi}}(h)]
.

Proof.

Fix a set
S
. For any subset
S
of coordinates, let the define the influence in
S
as
\Phi_{f,\chi}(\mathbf{x};S):=\sum_{i\in S}\Phi_{f,\chi}(\mathbf{x};i)
.
We are just summing the influences over the coordinates of
S
.

Consider the quantity
\mathbf{E}_{h\sim{\cal R}_{S}}[T_{\Phi_{\chi}}(h)]=\mathbf{E}_{h\sim{{\cal R}_{S}}}\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{z}}[\sqrt{\Phi_{h,\chi}(\mathbf{z})}]
.
Note that
\mathbf{z}
denotes a uar setting of the coordinates in
S
. The colorings of
h
are inherited from the coloring of
f
.
Each function
h
is indexed
by a (uar) setting of
\overline{S}
. Hence,

\mathbf{E}_{h\sim{{\cal R}_{S}}}\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{z}}[\sqrt{\Phi_{h,\chi}(\mathbf{z})}]=\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{x}}[\sqrt{\Phi_{f,\chi}(\mathbf{x};S)}]

(7)

The point
\mathbf{x}
is uar in the entire domain
[n]^{d}
.
Note that
\mathbf{E}_{S\sim{\cal H}(p)}[\Phi_{f,\chi}(\mathbf{x};S)]

is precisely
p\cdot\Phi_{f,\chi}(\mathbf{x};S)
, since each coordinate is independently picked in
S
with probability
p
.

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}_{S\sim{\cal H}(p)}\mathbf{E}_{h\sim{\cal R}_{S}}[T_{\Phi_{\chi}}(h)]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}_{S}\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{x}}[\sqrt{\Phi_{f,\chi}(\mathbf{x};S)}]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{x}}\mathbf{E}_{S}[\sqrt{\Phi_{f,\chi}(\mathbf{x};S)}]

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{x}}\Big[\sqrt{\mathbf{E}_{S}[\Phi_{f,\chi}(\mathbf{x};S)]}\Big]=\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{x}}\Big[\sqrt{p\cdot\Phi_{f,\chi}(\mathbf{x};S)}\Big]=\sqrt{p}\cdot T_{\Phi_{\chi}}(f)

The inequality above is a consequence of the concavity of the square root function and Jensen’s inequality.
∎

Now we have all the ingredients to prove
Theorem

3.3
whice we restate below for convenience.

See
3.3

Proof.

The proof is by induction over the dimension
d
of the domain.
Formally, we will prove a lower bound of
(C^{\prime}/10)\varepsilon
, where
C^{\prime}
is the constat of
Theorem
4.1
.

Let us first prove the base case, when
d\leq 10
.
Note that
\Phi_{f,\chi}(\mathbf{x})=\sum_{i=1}^{d}\Phi_{f,\chi}(\mathbf{x};i)
,
where each term in the summation is 0-1 valued. Hence,
by the
l_{1}
-
l_{2}
-inequality,
\sqrt{\Phi_{f,\chi}(\mathbf{x})}\geq\sum_{i=1}^{d}\Phi_{f,\chi}(\mathbf{x};i)/d=\Phi_{f,\chi}(\mathbf{x})/d
.

Thus,
T_{\Phi_{\chi}}(f)\geq\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{x}}[\Phi_{f,\chi}(\mathbf{x})]/d
. Furthermore,

\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{x}}[\Phi_{f,\chi}(\mathbf{x})]=\sum_{i=1}^{d}\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{x}}[\Phi_{f,\chi}(\mathbf{x};i)]
. We can
break the expectation over
\mathbf{x}
into lines as follows.

\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{x}}[\Phi_{f,\chi}(\mathbf{x})]=\sum_{i=1}^{d}\mathbf{E}_{\ell\ \textrm{uar $i$-line}}\mathbf{E}_{c}[\Phi_{{f|_{\ell}},\chi}(c)]

(The coordinate
c
is uar in
[n]
.) Now, for a Boolean function
f|_{\ell}
on a line, if the distance to monotonicity
is
\varepsilon
, then there are at least
\varepsilon n
violating pairs
[
EKK+00
]
, and thus for any coloring
\chi
, we have

\mathbf{E}_{c}[\Phi_{{f|_{\ell}},\chi}(c)]\geq\varepsilon(f|_{\ell})
,
and
\sum_{i=1}^{d}\mathbf{E}_{\ell\ \textrm{uar $i$-line}}\varepsilon(f|_{\ell})=\Omega(\varepsilon(f))
.

Hence,
T_{\Phi_{\chi}}(f)=\Omega(\varepsilon/d)
. For
d\leq 10
, the lemma holds, and so henceforth we assume
d\geq 10
.

Now for the induction step. We now break into cases.

Case 1,
\mathbf{E}_{\pi}\mathbf{E}_{S}[\Delta(\pi(S)\circ f,\pi(\overline{S})\circ f)]\geq\delta(f)/10
:
By
Theorem
4.1
,

T_{\Phi_{\chi}}(f)\geq c\cdot\mathbf{E}_{S}[\Delta(S\circ f,\overline{S}\circ f)]
(for any ordering of coordinates). So

T_{\Phi_{\chi}}(f)\geq c\cdot\mathbf{E}_{\pi}\mathbf{E}_{S}[\Delta(\pi(S)\circ f,\pi(\overline{S})\circ f)]\geq(c/10)\cdot\delta(f)
.

Case 2,
\mathbf{E}_{\pi}\mathbf{E}_{S}[\Delta(S\circ f,\overline{S}\circ f)]<\delta(f)/10
:
By
Claim
4.5
,

\mathbf{E}_{S}\mathbf{E}_{h\sim{\cal R}(S)}[\delta(h)]\geq\delta(f)-\mathbf{E}_{\pi}\mathbf{E}_{S}[\Delta(S\circ f,\overline{S}\circ f)]
.
In this case, we can lower bound
\mathbf{E}_{S}\mathbf{E}_{h\sim{\cal R}(S)}[\delta(h)]\geq(9/10)\delta(f)
.
Note that
S
is drawn from the distribution
{\cal H}(1/2)
.
When
S\neq[d]
, we can apply induction to
T_{\Phi_{\chi}}(h)
for
h\sim{\cal R}(S)
. Hence,

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}_{S\sim{\cal H}(1/2)}\mathbf{E}_{h\sim{\cal R}(S)}[T_{\Phi_{\chi}}(h)]

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle 2^{-d}\sum_{S\neq[d]}\mathbf{E}_{h\sim{\cal R}(S)}[T_{\Phi_{\chi}}(h)]\geq 2^{-d}\cdot(c/10)\cdot\sum_{S\neq[d]}\mathbf{E}_{h\sim{\cal R}(S)}[\delta(h)]

(8)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2^{-d}\cdot(c/10)\cdot\Big(\sum_{S\subseteq[d]}\mathbf{E}_{h\sim{\cal R}(S)}[\delta(h)]-\mathbf{E}_{h\sim{\cal R}([d])}[\delta(h)]\Big)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(c/10)\Big(\mathbf{E}_{S}\mathbf{E}_{h\sim{\cal R}(S)}[\delta(h)]-2^{-d}\delta(f)\Big)\ \ \ \ \textrm{($h\sim{\cal R}([d])$ is $f$)}

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle(c/10)\cdot(9/10)\cdot\delta(f)-2^{-d}\cdot(c/10)\cdot\delta(f)\ \ \ \textrm{(by case condition)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(9/10-2^{-d})\cdot(c/10)\cdot\delta(f)\geq(4/5)\cdot(c/10)\cdot\delta(f)

By
Claim
4.6
,
T_{\Phi_{\chi}}(f)\geq\sqrt{2}\cdot\mathbf{E}_{S\sim{\cal H}(1/2)}\mathbf{E}_{h\sim{\cal R}(S)}[T_{\Phi_{\chi}}(h)]
.
Combining with the inequality of
(
8
)
,
T_{\Phi_{\chi}}(f)\geq(\sqrt{2}\cdot 4/5)\cdot(c/10)\cdot\delta(f)\geq(c/10)\cdot\delta(f)
.
∎

5
Connecting Talagrand Objectives of
f
and the Tracker Functions

In this section and the next, we establish our main technical result
Theorem

3.2
relating the Talagrand objectives
on the colorful thresholded influence of the hypergrid function
f:[n]^{d}\to\{0,1\}
and the Talagrand objectives on the undirected influence of the
tracker functions. We restate the theorem below for convenience.

See
3.2

To prove
Theorem

3.2
we need to describe the coloring
\xi_{\mathbf{x}}
for each
\mathbf{x}
in
[n]^{d}
. We proceed doing so in
d
stages.

•

For every
i\in\{0,1,\ldots,d\}
and for every
\mathbf{x}\in[n]^{d}
, we define a
partial
edge coloring
\xi^{(i)}_{\mathbf{x}}
of the hypercube which assigns a
\{0,1\}
value to every hypercube edge of the form
(T,T\oplus j)
for all
j\leq i
, and for all
T\subseteq[i]
. The process will begin with the null coloring,
\xi^{(0)}_{\mathbf{x}}
, and end with a complete coloring,
\xi_{\mathbf{x}}:=\xi^{(d)}_{\mathbf{x}}
, for every
\mathbf{x}\in[n]^{d}
.

•

For every
i\in\{0,1,\ldots,d\}
and every
S\subseteq[i]
we will also define a coloring
\chi_{S}^{(i)}
of the edges of the augmented hypergrid. We start with
\chi^{(0)}_{\emptyset}:=\chi
where
\chi
is the original coloring which, recall, is adversarially chosen.

For every
i\in\{0,1,\ldots,d\}
and
S\subseteq[i]
we will use the above colorings to define the
(i,S)
-
hybrid Talagrand objective

R_{i}(S):=\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{x}\in[n]^{d}}\sqrt{~\sum_{j=1}^{i}I^{=j}_{g_{\mathbf{x}},\xi^{(i)}_{\mathbf{x}}}(S)~~+~~\sum_{j=i+1}^{d}\Phi_{S\circ f,\chi_{S}^{(i)}}(\mathbf{x};j)}\text{.}

(Colorful Hybrid)

Recall that
S\circ f
is the function obtained after sorting
f
on the coordinates in
S
. Note that
R_{i}(S)
is well-defined given the partial colorings
\xi_{\mathbf{x}}^{(i)}
for each
\mathbf{x}\in[n]^{d}
as defined above. Also
observe that since
\chi_{\emptyset}^{(0)}:=\chi
, the arbitrary coloring specified in the theorem statement, we have that

R_{0}(\emptyset)
is precisely the LHS in the statement of
Theorem

3.2
, that is,
R_{0}(\emptyset)=\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{x}\in[n]^{d}}~\left[\sqrt{\Phi_{f,\chi}(\mathbf{x})}\right]
. Additionally, since we use
\xi_{\mathbf{x}}:=\xi_{\mathbf{x}}^{(d)}
, observe that
\mathbf{E}_{S\subseteq[d]}[R_{d}(S)]
is precisely the RHS in the statement of
Theorem

3.2
.

With the above setup in mind, we show that the following
Lemma

5.1
suffices to prove
Theorem

3.2
.

Lemma 5.1

(Potential Drop Lemma)
.

Fix
i\in\{1,\ldots,d\}
,
\xi^{(i-1)}_{\mathbf{x}}
for all
\mathbf{x}\in[n]^{d}
, and
\chi_{S}^{(i-1)}
for every
S\subseteq[i-1]
, which all satisfy the specifications described in the previous paragraph.
There exists a choice of
\xi^{(i)}_{\mathbf{x}}
for every
\mathbf{x}\in[n]^{d}
and
\chi_{S}^{(i)}
,
\chi_{S+i}^{(i)}
for every
S\subseteq[i-1]
all satisfying the specifications described in the previous paragraph, such that for all
S\subseteq[i-1]
, we have (a)
R_{i-1}(S)\geq R_{i}(S)
and (b)
R_{i-1}(S)\geq R_{i}(S+i)
.

Proof of
Theorem

3.2
:

Consider the following binary tree with
d+1
levels. Each level
i\in\{0,1,\ldots,d\}
has
2^{i}
nodes indexed by subsets
S\subseteq[i]
.
Every such node is associated with a coloring
\chi_{S}^{(i)}
of the augmented hypergrid edges. The level
i
is also associated with a partial coloring
\xi^{(i)}_{\mathbf{x}}
for every
\mathbf{x}\in[n]^{d}
.

The
0
’th level contains a single node indexed by
\emptyset
. The associated augmented hypergrid coloring
is
\chi_{\emptyset}^{(0)}:=\chi
. The partial coloring
\xi^{(0)}_{\mathbf{x}}
is null for all
\mathbf{x}\in[n]^{d}
.
We associate the value
R_{0}(\emptyset)=T_{\Phi_{\chi}}(f)
with the root.

For
1\leq i\leq d
, we describe the children of each node in level
i-1
. Each node in level
i-1
is indexed by some
S\subseteq[i-1]
. We associate this node with the value
R_{i-1}(S)
. This node has two children at level
i
: one, the left child, indexed by
S
and the other, the right child, indexed by
S+i
. The coloring of the hypergrid edges at the left child is defined as
\chi_{S}^{(i)}
from the lemma, and that of the hypergrid edges at the right child is defined as
\chi_{S+i}^{(i)}
from the lemma. The left and right children hold the quantites
R_{i}(S)
and
R_{i}(S+i)
, respectively. At level
i
, the partial coloring
\xi_{\mathbf{x}}^{(i-1)}
is also extended to
\xi_{\mathbf{x}}^{(i)}
for every
\mathbf{x}\in[n]^{d}
as stated in the lemma. From the lemma, we have
R_{i-1}(S)\geq R_{i}(S)
and
R_{i-1}(S)\geq R_{i}(S+i)
. This immediately implies the following:

\text{For all }i\in\{1,\ldots,d\}\text{, we have }\mathbf{E}_{S\subseteq[i-1]}[R_{i-1}(S)]\geq\mathbf{E}_{S\subseteq[i]}[R_{i}(S)]

and chaining these
d
inequalities together yields
R_{0}(\emptyset)\geq\mathbf{E}_{S\subseteq[d]}[R_{d}(S)]
.

Now consider the leaf nodes of this tree, which hold the values
R_{d}(S)
for every
S\subseteq[d]
. Observe that
R_{d}(S)=\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{x}\in[n]^{d}}\left[\sqrt{I_{g_{\mathbf{x}},\xi_{\mathbf{x}}}(S)}\right]
since
\xi_{\mathbf{x}}:=\xi_{\mathbf{x}}^{(d)}
. Recalling that
R_{0}(\emptyset)=T_{\Phi_{\chi}}(f)
yields

T_{\Phi_{\chi}}(f)=R_{0}(\emptyset)\geq\mathbf{E}_{S\subseteq[d]}[R_{d}(S)]=\mathbf{E}_{S\subseteq[d]}\mathbf{E}_{\mathbf{x}\in[n]^{d}}\left[\sqrt{I_{g_{\mathbf{x}},\xi_{\mathbf{x}}}(S)}\right]

and this establishes the claim after exchanging the expectations. ∎

6
Proof of Potential Drop
Lemma

5.1

Recall
i\in\{1,\ldots,d\}
is fixed. For brevity’s sake, we will fix a set
S\subseteq[i-1]
and call
h:=(S\circ f)
. Let’s refer to
\chi^{(i-1)}_{S}
as simply
\chi
without confusing with the original
\chi
in the theorem.
The two colorings
\chi^{(i)}_{S}
and
\chi^{(i)}_{S+i}
that we construct will be simply called
\chi^{\prime}
and
\chi^{\prime\prime}
, respectively.
Let’s call the partial colorings
\xi^{(i-1)}_{\mathbf{x}}
as simply
\xi_{\mathbf{x}}
.
We will call the coloring
\xi^{(i)}_{\mathbf{x}}
which we need to construct simply
\xi^{\prime}_{\mathbf{x}}
in the latter. Recall that
\xi_{\mathbf{x}}
is defined on all edges
(T,T\oplus j)
for

and
j\leq i-1
and in order to prove the lemma we will need to define
\xi_{\mathbf{x}}^{\prime}
on all edges
(T\oplus j)
for
T\subseteq[i]
and
j\leq i
.

Fix an
i
-line
\ell
. We prove the lemma line-by-line. To be precise, let us consider the following vectors. First,

A Picture of the Line.

Since
h
is semisorted, the picture of
h
restricted to
\ell
looks like this. The green zone is where the function is
1
.
Without loss of generality we assume
\ell
has more ones than zeros.
We use
A
to denote the ones on the left and
C
to denote the zeros on the right. We use
k:=|C|
, and
B\subseteq A
are the
k
right most ones
in the left side.

Throughout, we will use the notation
\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\displaystyle\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern defined on

’s will be always picked from

or unions of these, always making sure they form a contiguous interval.

High Level Idea.

Before we venture into proving the inequalities, we would like to remind the reader again
of the proof strategy discussed in
Section

1.3
. We need to define the colorings
\chi^{\prime}
,
\chi^{\prime\prime}
, and
also
\xi_{\mathbf{x}}^{(i)}
’s such that the objective after recoloring satisfy the inequality we desire to prove. This going to hinge upon showing that the vector
obtained after operation either majorizes or is coordinate-wise dominated by a vector that majorizes the vector before the operation.
In particular, these are the conditions (a)-(d) and (e)-(h) mentioned below in the grey boxes.
To show these properties, we would be crucially using the property that the function
f
is semi-sorted which leads to certain monotonicity properties that allows us to claim them. In particular, we would be using
Lemma

2.11
when establishing almost all the conditions mentioned above.
There is a certain sense of repetition in which these arguments are made, however, we have provided all the details for completeness.

6.1
Proving
\mathsf{LHS}\geq\mathsf{RHS}_{1}

During the proof of
\mathsf{LHS}\geq\mathsf{RHS}_{1}
, we will define the coloring
\chi^{\prime}
on all edges of the fully augmented hypergrid and
\xi^{\prime}_{\mathbf{x}}(S,S\oplus j)
where
j\leq i
for all
\mathbf{x}\in[n]^{d}
.
We will not specify
\xi^{\prime}_{\mathbf{x}}(S+i,S+i\oplus j)
since these won’t be needed to prove this inequality; we will describe them when we prove
\mathsf{LHS}\geq\mathsf{RHS}_{2}
.

Before we describe the recolorings, it is useful to describe the plan of the proof. This will motivate why we recolor as we do.
We will actually consider

0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle L^{\prime(2)}_{A}\hfil$\crcr}}}\right\rVert_{1/2}
.
Part (a) and the first part of (b), along with
Lemma

2.11
, implies
\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\displaystyle\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\textstyle R^{(1)}_{A}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mkern 2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern 2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle L^{\prime(2)}_{A}\hfil$\crcr}}}\succeq_{\mathsf{maj}}\left(\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\displaystyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle L_{A}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\textstyle L_{A}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptstyle L_{A}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle L_{A}\hfil$\crcr}}}\right)^{\downarrow}
.
And so,
\left\lVert L_{A}\right\rVert_{1/2}\geq\left\lVert R_{A}\right\rVert_{1/2}
. A similar argument using (c) and (d) implies
\left\lVert L_{C}\right\rVert_{1/2}\geq\left\lVert R_{C}\right\rVert_{1/2}
.

One last observation is needed to complete the proof. Note that
R^{(2)}_{W}
is the
zero
vector: the points
x\in W
don’t change value even when
\ell
is sorted.
Also note that
L^{(2)}_{W}
is the zero vector; the points
x\in W
don’t participate in a violation in direction
i
.
And therefore, part (c) along with
Lemma

2.11
implies
\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\displaystyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle R_{W}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\textstyle R_{W}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptstyle R_{W}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle R_{W}\hfil$\crcr}}}\succeq_{\mathsf{maj}}\left(\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\displaystyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle L_{W}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\textstyle L_{W}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptstyle L_{W}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle L_{W}\hfil$\crcr}}}\right)^{\uparrow}
implying
\left\lVert L_{W}\right\rVert_{1/2}\geq\left\lVert R_{W}\right\rVert_{1/2}
.
Similarly,
R^{(2)}_{O}\equiv L^{(2)}_{O}\equiv\mathbf{0}
, and thus part (a) along with
Lemma

2.11
implies
\left\lVert L_{O}\right\rVert_{1/2}\geq\left\lVert R_{O}\right\rVert_{1/2}
.

6.1.1
Proving (a) and (c) for
q=3

Defining the Coloring
\chi^{\prime}
:

We will now describe the coloring
\chi^{\prime}
on all edges of the form
(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{x}+a\mathbf{e}_{j})
where
j\geq i+1
,
h(\mathbf{x})=1
and
h(\mathbf{x}+a\mathbf{e}_{j})=0
. For all other edges
e
, we simply define
\chi^{\prime}(e)=\chi(e)
as these edges do not play a role in proving the inequality.

Given a pair of
i
-lines
\ell
and
\ell^{\prime}=\ell+a\mathbf{e}_{j}
for
j\geq i+1
and
a>0
, we consider the set of violations from
\ell
to
\ell^{\prime}
in
h
:

\displaystyle V:=\{(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{x}+a\mathbf{e}_{j})\colon\mathbf{x}\in\ell\text{, }h(\mathbf{x})=1\text{, and }h(\mathbf{x}+a\mathbf{e}_{j})=0\}\text{.}

(15)

Since
h
is semi-sorted, it’s clear that we can write
V=V_{L}\cup V_{R}
as a union of two intervals, in the sense that
\{\mathbf{x}\colon(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{x}+a\mathbf{e}_{j})\in V_{L}\}
is an interval in the lower half of
\ell
and
\{\mathbf{x}\colon(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{x}+a\mathbf{e}_{j})\in V_{R}\}
is an interval in the upper half of
\ell
. Similarly, the upper endpoints form two intervals in
\ell^{\prime}
. We then obtain
\chi^{\prime}
by down-sorting
\chi
on each of these intervals, moving left-to-right:

(\chi^{\prime}(e)\colon e\in V_{L})=\left(\chi(e)\colon e\in V_{L}\right)^{\downarrow}\text{ and }(\chi^{\prime}(e)\colon e\in V_{R})=\left(\chi(e)\colon e\in V_{R}\right)^{\downarrow}\text{.}

We provide the following illustration for clarity. The white and green intervals represent where
h=0
and
h=1
, respectively. The vertical arrows represent violated edges. Blue edges have color
0
and red edges have color
1
. The left picture depicts the original coloring,
\chi
, and the right picture depicts the recoloring
\chi^{\prime}
.

We now return to our fixed
i
-line
\ell
and set out to prove parts (a) and (c) for
q=3
, given this coloring
\chi^{\prime}
. Let’s recall our illustration of
\ell
and our definition of the intervals
W,A,C,O
.

Proving (a) for
q=3
:

Fix
j\geq i+1
and a
i
-line
\ell^{\prime}:=\ell+a\mathbf{e}_{j}
. Let
A^{\prime}:=\{\mathbf{x}\in A~:~h(\mathbf{x}+a\mathbf{e}_{j})=0\}
and
O^{\prime}:=\{\mathbf{x}\in O~:~h(\mathbf{x}+a\mathbf{e}_{j})=0\}
. Since
h
is semi-sorted, it is not hard to see that
A^{\prime}
and
O^{\prime}
are prefixes of
A
and
O
, respectively.

Claim 6.1
.

If
\mathbf{x}_{i}<\mathbf{x}^{\prime}_{i}
in
A
such that
\mathbf{x}^{\prime}\in A^{\prime}
, then
\mathbf{x}\in A^{\prime}
. The same is true for
O
and
O^{\prime}
.

Proof.

Since
h
is semisorted,
h(\mathbf{x}^{\prime}+a\mathbf{e}_{j})=0
implies
h(\mathbf{x}+a\mathbf{e}_{j})=0
.
∎

Moreover, observe that our definition of
\chi^{\prime}
gives us

(\chi^{\prime}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{x}+a\mathbf{e}_{j})\colon\mathbf{x}\in A^{\prime})=\left(\chi(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{x}+a\mathbf{e}_{j})\colon\mathbf{x}\in A^{\prime}\right)^{\downarrow}

and

(\chi^{\prime}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{x}+a\mathbf{e}_{j})\colon\mathbf{x}\in O^{\prime})=\left(\chi(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{x}+a\mathbf{e}_{j})\colon\mathbf{x}\in O^{\prime}\right)^{\downarrow}\text{.}

Let’s investigate what this leads to. These are key properties.

Definition 6.2
.

Fix
j\geq i+1
and fix an
i
-line
\ell^{\prime}:=\ell+a\mathbf{e}_{j}
for
a>0
. Define the following two boolean vectors

\mathbf{v}^{R}_{j,a}:=\left({\bf 1}{\left(h(\mathbf{x}+a\mathbf{e}_{j})=0~~\textbf{and}~~~\chi^{\prime}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{x}+a\mathbf{e}_{j})=1\right)}~~:~\mathbf{x}\in A\right)

and

\mathbf{v}^{L}_{j,a}:=\left({\bf 1}{\left(h(\mathbf{x}+a\mathbf{e}_{j})=0~~\textbf{and}~~~\chi(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{x}+a\mathbf{e}_{j})=1\right)}~~:~\mathbf{x}\in A\right)

Observe, for
\mathbf{x}\in A
,

\Phi_{h,\chi^{\prime}}(\mathbf{x};j)=\min\left(1,\sum_{a}\mathbf{v}^{R}_{j,a}(\mathbf{x})\right)~~\textrm{and}~~~\Phi_{h,\chi}(\mathbf{x};j)=\min\left(1,\sum_{a}\mathbf{v}^{L}_{j,a}(\mathbf{x})\right)

(16)

Claim 6.3
.

Fix a
j\geq i+1
and
a>0
. For any two
\mathbf{x}_{i}<\mathbf{x}^{\prime}_{i}
in
A
, we have
\mathbf{v}^{R}_{j,a}(\mathbf{x})\geq\mathbf{v}^{R}_{j,a}(\mathbf{x}^{\prime})
.
That is, the vector
\mathbf{v}^{R}_{j,a}
is sorted decreasing.

Proof.

Since
h
is semisorted
h(\mathbf{x}^{\prime}+a\mathbf{e}_{j})=0
implies
h(\mathbf{x}+a\mathbf{e}_{j})=0
.
Furthermore, since both these are violations, by design
\chi^{\prime}(\mathbf{x}^{\prime},\mathbf{x}^{\prime}+a\mathbf{e}_{j})=1
implies
\chi^{\prime}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{x}+a\mathbf{e}_{j})=1
.
∎

Claim 6.4
.

Fix a
j\geq i+1
and
a>0
. The vectors
\mathbf{v}^{R}_{j,a}
and
\mathbf{v}^{L}_{j,a}
are permutations of one another.

Proof.

This is precisely how
\chi^{\prime}
is defined: it only permutes the colorings on the violations incident on
A
.
∎

In conclusion, using the observation (
16
), we conclude that we can write

\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\displaystyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle L^{(3)}_{A}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\textstyle L^{(3)}_{A}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptstyle L^{(3)}_{A}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle L^{(3)}_{A}\hfil$\crcr}}}=\left(\sum_{j=i+1}^{d}\Phi_{h,\chi}(\mathbf{x};j)~~:~~\mathbf{x}\in A\right)

as a weighted sum of Boolean vectors, and the above two claims imply that the vector

\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle R^{(3)}_{A}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\textstyle R^{(3)}_{A}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern is the same weighted sum of the
sorted decreasing
orders of those Boolean vectors.
Therefore, we can conclude using
Lemma

2.11
,

\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\textstyle R^{(3)}_{A}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern (17)

(18)

Proving (c) for
q=3
:

The picture is similar, but reversed, when we consider the points in
W\cup C
, where
h(\mathbf{x})=0
. Recall the definition of
W
and
C
as in the illustration. Fix
j\geq i+1
and a
i
-line
\ell^{\prime\prime}:=\ell-a\mathbf{e}_{j}
. Let
W^{\prime}:=\{\mathbf{x}\in W~:~h(\mathbf{x}-a\mathbf{e}_{j})=1\}
and
C^{\prime}:=\{\mathbf{x}\in C~:~h(\mathbf{x}-a\mathbf{e}_{j})=1\}
. It is not hard to see that
W^{\prime}
and
C^{\prime}
are suffixes of
W
and
C
, respectively.

Claim 6.5
.

If
\mathbf{x}_{i}<\mathbf{x}^{\prime}_{i}
in
W
such that
\mathbf{x}\in W^{\prime}
, then
\mathbf{x}^{\prime}\in W^{\prime}
. The same is true for
C
and
C^{\prime}
.

Proof.

Since
h
is semisorted,
h(\mathbf{x}-a\mathbf{e}_{j})=1
implies
h(\mathbf{x}^{\prime}-a\mathbf{e}_{j})=1
.
∎

Again, observe that our definition of
\chi^{\prime}
gives us

(\chi^{\prime}(\mathbf{x}-a\mathbf{e}_{j},\mathbf{x})\colon\mathbf{x}\in W^{\prime})=\left(\chi(\mathbf{x}-a\mathbf{e}_{j},\mathbf{x})\colon\mathbf{x}\in W^{\prime}\right)^{\downarrow}

and

(\chi^{\prime}(\mathbf{x}-a\mathbf{e}_{j},\mathbf{x})\colon\mathbf{x}\in C^{\prime})=\left(\chi(\mathbf{x}-a\mathbf{e}_{j},\mathbf{x})\colon\mathbf{x}\in C^{\prime}\right)^{\downarrow}

Definition 6.6
.

Fix
j\geq i+1
and fix an
i
-line
\ell^{\prime\prime}:=\ell-a\mathbf{e}_{j}
for
a>0
. Define the following two boolean vectors

\mathbf{v}^{R}_{j,a}:=\left({\bf 1}{\left(h(\mathbf{x}-a\mathbf{e}_{j})=1~~\textbf{and}~~~\chi^{\prime}(\mathbf{x}-a\mathbf{e}_{j},\mathbf{x})=0\right)}~~:~\mathbf{x}\in C\right)

and

\mathbf{v}^{L}_{j,a}:=\left({\bf 1}{\left(h(\mathbf{x}-a\mathbf{e}_{j})=1~~\textbf{and}~~~\chi(\mathbf{x}-a\mathbf{e}_{j},\mathbf{x})=0\right)}~~:~\mathbf{x}\in C\right)

Observe, for
\mathbf{x}\in C
,

\Phi_{h,\chi^{\prime}}(\mathbf{x};j)=\min\left(1,\sum_{a}\mathbf{v}^{R}_{j,a}(\mathbf{x})\right)~~\textrm{and}~~~\Phi_{h,\chi}(\mathbf{x};j)=\min\left(1,\sum_{a}\mathbf{v}^{L}_{j,a}(\mathbf{x})\right)

(19)

Claim 6.7
.

Fix a
j\geq i+1
and
a>0
. For any two
\mathbf{x}_{i}>\mathbf{x}^{\prime}_{i}
in
C
, we have
\mathbf{v}^{R}_{j,a}(\mathbf{x})\geq\mathbf{v}^{R}_{j,a}(\mathbf{x}^{\prime})
.
That is, the vector
\mathbf{v}^{R}_{j,a}
is sorted increasing when considered left to right.

Proof.

Since
h
is semisorted
h(\mathbf{x}^{\prime}-a\mathbf{e}_{j})=1
implies
h(\mathbf{x}-a\mathbf{e}_{j})=1
.
Furthermore, since both these are violations, by design
\chi^{\prime}(\mathbf{x}^{\prime},\mathbf{x}^{\prime}+a\mathbf{e}_{j})=0
implies
\chi^{\prime}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{x}+a\mathbf{e}_{j})=0
.
∎

Claim 6.8
.

Fix a
j\geq i+1
and
a>0
. The vectors
\mathbf{v}^{R}_{j,a}
and
\mathbf{v}^{L}_{j,a}
are permutations of one another.

A similar argument to the one given above now implies

\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr

\hfil\textstyle L^{(3)}_{C}\hfil

is a sum of Boolean vectors, and

\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr

\hfil\textstyle R^{(3)}_{C}\hfil

is the sum of the
sorted increasing
orders of those Boolean vectors.
Using
Lemma

2.11
, we can conclude

\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern And an absolutely analogous argument gives

2.0mu\scriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptstyle R^{(3)}_{W}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle R^{(3)}_{W}\hfil$\crcr}}}\succeq_{\mathsf{maj}}\left(\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle L^{(3)}_{W}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern (21)

This finishes the proofs of
q=3
for (a) and (c).

6.1.2
Proving (a) and (c) for
q=1

Defining
\xi^{\prime}_{\mathbf{x}}(S,S\oplus j)
for
S\subseteq[i-1]
and
j\leq i-1
:

We now define the partial coloring
\xi^{\prime}_{\mathbf{x}}:=\xi^{(i)}_{\mathbf{x}}
on all edges
(S,S\oplus j)
where
S\subseteq[i-1]
and
j\leq i-1
for all
\mathbf{x}\in[n]^{d}
. These are exactly the relevant edges for the proof of parts (a) and (c) for
q=1
. Note that the partial coloring
\xi_{\mathbf{x}}:=\xi_{\mathbf{x}}^{(i-1)}
is defined over precisely these edges for each
\mathbf{x}\in[n]^{d}
. The color of
\xi^{\prime}_{\mathbf{x}}
on the edges
(S,S+i)
for
S\subseteq[i-1]
will be defined when we prove parts (b) and (d). The color of
\xi^{\prime}_{\mathbf{x}}
on the edges
(S+i,S+i\oplus j)
for
S\subseteq[i-1]
and
j\leq i-1
will be defined when we prove
\mathsf{LHS}\geq\mathsf{RHS}_{2}
.

Fix
j\leq i-1
,
S\subseteq[i-1]
, and a
i
-line
\ell
. We consider the set of
\mathbf{x}\in\ell
such that
(S,S\oplus j)
is influential in
g_{\mathbf{x}}
:

\displaystyle V:=\left\{\mathbf{x}\in\ell\colon g_{\mathbf{x}}(S)=1\text{ and }g_{\mathbf{x}}(S\oplus j)=0\right\}\text{.}

(22)

Note that since
f
is semi-sorted, we have that
(S\circ f)
and
(S\oplus j\circ f)
are both semi-sorted. Thus, we can write
V=V_{L}\cup V_{R}
where
V_{L}
and
V_{R}
are intervals contained in the left and right half of
\ell
, respectively. We again obtain
\xi^{\prime}_{\mathbf{x}}
by down-sorting the original coloring on these intervals:

(\xi^{\prime}_{\mathbf{x}}(S,S\oplus j)\colon\mathbf{x}\in V_{L})=\left(\xi_{\mathbf{x}}(S,S\oplus j)\colon\mathbf{x}\in V_{L}\right)^{\downarrow}

and similarly

(\xi^{\prime}_{\mathbf{x}}(S,S\oplus j)\colon\mathbf{x}\in V_{R})=\left(\xi_{\mathbf{x}}(S,S\oplus j)\colon\mathbf{x}\in V_{R}\right)^{\downarrow}\text{.}

For all
\mathbf{x}\in\ell\setminus V
, we define
\xi^{\prime}_{\mathbf{x}}(S,S\oplus j):=\xi_{\mathbf{x}}(S,S\oplus j)
. This completely describes
\xi^{\prime}_{\mathbf{x}}(S,S\oplus j)
for every
\mathbf{x}\in[n]^{d}
.

We provide the following illustration for clarity. Note that the picture is quite similar to the one provided in
Section

6.1.1
, when we defined
\chi^{\prime}
. The key difference is that the bottom and top segments represent the same line
\ell
, but with different functions
S\circ f
and
(S\oplus j)\circ f
, respectively. The vertical lines are no longer arrows to emphasize that they represent
undirected edges in the hypercube
as opposed to directed edges in the augmented hypergrid.

We now return to our fixed
i
-line
\ell
and set out to prove parts (a) and (c) for
q=1
, given the colorings
\xi^{\prime}_{\mathbf{x}}
. Let’s recall our illustration of
\ell
and our definition of the intervals
W,A,C,O
. Recall that
g_{\mathbf{x}}=h(\mathbf{x})
and so the definition of these intervals is the same.

Proof of Part (a) for
q=1
:

Fix
j\leq i-1
and let
A^{\prime}=\{\mathbf{x}\in A\colon g_{\mathbf{x}}(S\oplus j)=0\}
and
O^{\prime}=\{\mathbf{x}\in O\colon g_{\mathbf{x}}(S\oplus j)=0\}
, which are prefixes of
A
and
O
, respectively. From our definition of
\xi^{\prime}_{\mathbf{x}}(S,S\oplus j)
above, we have

(\xi^{\prime}_{\mathbf{x}}(S,S\oplus j)\colon\mathbf{x}\in A^{\prime})=\left(\xi_{\mathbf{x}}(S,S\oplus j)\colon\mathbf{x}\in A^{\prime}\right)^{\downarrow}

and similarly

(\xi^{\prime}_{\mathbf{x}}(S,S\oplus j)\colon\mathbf{x}\in O^{\prime})=\left(\xi_{\mathbf{x}}(S,S\oplus j)\colon\mathbf{x}\in O^{\prime}\right)^{\downarrow}\text{.}

Claim 6.9
.

\left(I_{g_{\mathbf{x}},\xi^{\prime}_{\mathbf{x}}}^{=j}(S)~~:~~\mathbf{x}\in A\right)
is a sorted decreasing vector, and is a permutation of
\left(I_{g_{\mathbf{x}},\xi_{\mathbf{x}}}^{=j}(S)~~:~~\mathbf{x}\in A\right)
.

Proof.

Take
\mathbf{x}_{i}<\mathbf{x}^{\prime}_{i}
in
A
. Note that
g_{\mathbf{x}}(S)=1
for both
\mathbf{x},\mathbf{x}^{\prime}
. Thus,

I_{g_{\mathbf{x}},\xi^{\prime}_{\mathbf{x}}}^{=j}(S)={\bf 1}\left(g_{\mathbf{x}}(S\oplus j)=0~\textbf{and}~\xi^{\prime}_{\mathbf{x}}(S,S\oplus j)=1\right)

and

I_{g_{\mathbf{x}},\xi_{\mathbf{x}}}^{=j}(S)={\bf 1}\left(g_{\mathbf{x}}(S\oplus j)=0~\textbf{and}~\xi_{\mathbf{x}}(S,S\oplus j)=1\right)

The two vectors are Boolean vectors with number of ones equal to the number of ones in
(\xi_{\mathbf{x}}(S,S\oplus j)~:~\mathbf{x}\in A^{\prime})
which equals
the number of ones in
(\xi^{\prime}_{\mathbf{x}}(S,S\oplus j)~:~\mathbf{x}\in A^{\prime})
. Thus, they are permutations.
By design of
\xi^{\prime}_{\mathbf{x}}
’s, this vector is sorted decreasing on
A^{\prime}
, and all zeros in
A\setminus A^{\prime}
(which come to the right of
A^{\prime}
).
∎

Proof of Part (c) for
q=1
:

The picture is similar, but reversed when we consider the points in

, where
g_{\mathbf{x}}(S)=0
. Fix
j\leq i-1
and define
W^{\prime}:=\{\mathbf{x}\in W\colon g_{\mathbf{x}}(S\oplus j)=1\}
and
C^{\prime}:=\{\mathbf{x}\in C\colon g_{\mathbf{x}}(S\oplus j)=1\}
which are suffixes of
W
and
C
, respectively. From our definition of
\xi^{\prime}_{\mathbf{x}}(S,S\oplus j)
above, made from the perspective of the set
S\oplus j
, we have

(\xi^{\prime}_{\mathbf{x}}(S,S\oplus j)\colon\mathbf{x}\in W^{\prime})=\left(\xi_{\mathbf{x}}(S,S\oplus j)\colon\mathbf{x}\in W^{\prime}\right)^{\downarrow}

and similarly

(\xi^{\prime}_{\mathbf{x}}(S,S\oplus j)\colon\mathbf{x}\in C^{\prime})=\left(\xi_{\mathbf{x}}(S,S\oplus j)\colon\mathbf{x}\in C^{\prime}\right)^{\downarrow}\text{.}

Analogous to
6.9
, we have the following claim.

Claim 6.10
.

\left(I_{g_{\mathbf{x}},\xi^{\prime}_{\mathbf{x}}}^{=j}(S)~~:~~\mathbf{x}\in W\right)
is a sorted increasing vector, and is a permutation of
\left(I_{g_{\mathbf{x}},\xi_{\mathbf{x}}}^{=j}(S)~~:~~\mathbf{x}\in W\right)
.

Equation

23
